A little, yeah… I don’t want to make it sound like I’m refusing to do my job or anything. But since they made me the acting workflow lead, I think I’ve been putting off some of the stuff that feels like it could go wrong.

I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing I should be coming in for, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not as much when I’m away from work, I don’t think. It’s more like… it starts before I even get there, like I’m already tense walking into the warehouse because of the workflow lead thing. Then I’ll get wound up over small SAP errors, and I hate that because I know it probably sounds minor. \n\nBy the time I’m home I’m mostly just drained, and I haven’t really had the energy for basketball or sketching. So I guess it’s not constant anxiety everywhere, but it’s definitely carrying over some, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes or stressful events in your personal life recently, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I mean, nothing big that I can think of outside of work. Home life is pretty normal, as far as I can tell. I don’t want to overstate it—there hasn’t been, like, a major event or anything. It mostly seems tied to the work change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since you became the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the time, no. I mean, I’ve been frustrated and kind of worn out by it, but “sad” feels maybe stronger than what I’d call it. Mostly I just feel tense about work and then tired afterward… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I don’t want to make it sound like I’m refusing to do my job or anything. But since they made me the acting workflow lead, I think I’ve been putting off some of the stuff that feels like it could go wrong.\n\nLike cycle counts—I used to stay on top of those, and now I’ll kind of delay getting into them, and then I end up missing deadlines I wouldn’t have missed before. And with SAP issues, even small errors get me really tense, so I probably avoid dealing with them right away sometimes. I know that’s not ideal.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach issues since becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might get the normal kind of tension now and then, but I haven’t noticed headaches or stomach problems in any real way since taking that role. Nothing I’d want to make a big deal out of, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family since you started feeling this way about work?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, I might be a little quieter when I get home, but I don’t think it’s caused any real problems with friends or family. At least, nobody’s said anything to me. I’m trying not to take work home too much, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of your work-related stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a big way. I mean, sometimes I might lie there thinking about work a little, but I wouldn’t say I’m having real sleep problems because of it. At least nothing that feels serious, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate on things you normally enjoy, like basketball or sketching, when you are doing them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so, a little more than I’d like to admit. I haven’t really been showing up for basketball lately, and I’ve let my usual sketching time kind of disappear. It’s not that I don’t enjoy them anymore, it’s more like work keeps taking over my headspace and my time.\n\nAnd, um… my supervisor did bring up some missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, which was embarrassing. I know that’s not exactly the same as concentrating on hobbies, but it feels related, like I’m not keeping up the way I normally would.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

And with SAP issues, even small errors get me really tense, so I probably avoid dealing with them right away sometimes. I know that’s not ideal.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach issues since becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might get the normal kind of tension now and then, but I haven’t noticed headaches or stomach problems in any real way since taking that role. Nothing I’d want to make a big deal out of, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family since you started feeling this way about work?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, I might be a little quieter when I get home, but I don’t think it’s caused any real problems with friends or family. At least, nobody’s said anything to me. I’m trying not to take work home too much, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of your work-related stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a big way. I mean, sometimes I might lie there thinking about work a little, but I wouldn’t say I’m having real sleep problems because of it. At least nothing that feels serious, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate on things you normally enjoy, like basketball or sketching, when you are doing them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so, a little more than I’d like to admit. I haven’t really been showing up for basketball lately, and I’ve let my usual sketching time kind of disappear. It’s not that I don’t enjoy them anymore, it’s more like work keeps taking over my headspace and my time.\n\nAnd, um… my supervisor did bring up some missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, which was embarrassing. I know that’s not exactly the same as concentrating on hobbies, but it feels related, like I’m not keeping up the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work-related stress is affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is, more than I wanted to admit at first. I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, because it’s not, but work has started taking up a lot of space in my life.\n\nMy supervisor had to bring up those missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, which was hard to hear. And outside of work, I’ve kind of stopped showing up for basketball, and my usual sketching time has pretty much disappeared. It feels like work is sort of taking over, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I think it’s mostly the responsibilities themselves… but, yeah, tied up with not wanting to mess it up, too.

Ever since they made me the acting workflow lead, I kind of walk into the warehouse already tense. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s been following me home. I’m too drained for basketball, and I haven’t really been working on my sketches. So… I guess it’s both, but it started with the added responsibility. Does that make sense?

Then by the time I get home, I’m just drained… not really playing basketball or working on my sketches like I used to. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing I should be coming in for, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not as much when I’m away from work, I don’t think. It’s more like… it starts before I even get there, like I’m already tense walking into the warehouse because of the workflow lead thing. Then I’ll get wound up over small SAP errors, and I hate that because I know it probably sounds minor. \n\nBy the time I’m home I’m mostly just drained, and I haven’t really had the energy for basketball or sketching. I mean, nothing big that I can think of outside of work. Home life is pretty normal, as far as I can tell. I don’t want to overstate it—there hasn’t been, like, a major event or anything. I mean, I’ve been frustrated and kind of worn out by it, but “sad” feels maybe stronger than what I’d call it. Mostly I just feel tense about work and then tired afterward… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I don’t want to make it sound like I’m refusing to do my job or anything. But since they made me the acting workflow lead, I think I’ve been putting off some of the stuff that feels like it could go wrong.\n\nLike cycle counts—I used to stay on top of those, and now I’ll kind of delay getting into them, and then I end up missing deadlines I wouldn’t have missed before. Yeah… I think it is causing significant distress. I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s kind of been there all the time since they made me acting workflow lead.

It feels like work is just… taking over more of my life than it should. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to fulfill your role as acting workflow lead due to your stress?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m completely unable to do it… I mean, I’m still showing up and trying to handle the role. But it’s definitely affecting me.\n\nMy supervisor did have to bring up the missed cycle-count deadlines during a performance check-in, which was hard to hear. And outside of work, I’ve kind of stopped showing up for basketball and I haven’t been making time to sketch like I usually do, because it feels like work is taking over most of my headspace.\n\nSo… maybe not unable, exactly, but I’m not keeping up the way I normally would. I mean… I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people completely or anything. But yeah, I guess I’ve pulled back some.

I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing I should be coming in for, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not as much when I’m away from work, I don’t think. It’s more like… it starts before I even get there, like I’m already tense walking into the warehouse because of the workflow lead thing. Then I’ll get wound up over small SAP errors, and I hate that because I know it probably sounds minor. \n\nBy the time I’m home I’m mostly just drained, and I haven’t really had the energy for basketball or sketching. Mostly I just feel tense about work and then tired afterward… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I don’t want to make it sound like I’m refusing to do my job or anything. But since they made me the acting workflow lead, I think I’ve been putting off some of the stuff that feels like it could go wrong.\n\nLike cycle counts—I used to stay on top of those, and now I’ll kind of delay getting into them, and then I end up missing deadlines I wouldn’t have missed before. And with SAP issues, even small errors get me really tense, so I probably avoid dealing with them right away sometimes. I know that’s not ideal.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach issues since becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might get the normal kind of tension now and then, but I haven’t noticed headaches or stomach problems in any real way since taking that role. Nothing I’d want to make a big deal out of, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family since you started feeling this way about work?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, I might be a little quieter when I get home, but I don’t think it’s caused any real problems with friends or family. At least, nobody’s said anything to me. I’m trying not to take work home too much, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of your work-related stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a big way. I mean, sometimes I might lie there thinking about work a little, but I wouldn’t say I’m having real sleep problems because of it. At least nothing that feels serious, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate on things you normally enjoy, like basketball or sketching, when you are doing them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so, a little more than I’d like to admit. I haven’t really been showing up for basketball lately, and I’ve let my usual sketching time kind of disappear. I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, because it’s not, but work has started taking up a lot of space in my life.\n\nMy supervisor had to bring up those missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, which was hard to hear. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess I have started doubting myself a bit. I don’t love admitting that, because I’ve always felt like I was pretty dependable at work.

But since taking on the acting workflow lead role, I’ve missed some cycle-count deadlines, and my supervisor had to bring that up during a performance check-in. That kind of shook me. It made me wonder if I’m actually handling the job the way I should be.

And then outside of work, I’m not really doing the things that usually help me feel like myself — I’ve stopped showing up for basketball and I’m not making time to sketch. It feels like work is taking up so much space in my life that it makes me question whether I’m keeping up okay. Is that… does that answer what you’re asking?

I find myself getting irritated over little SAP errors and stuff like that, which isn’t really how I like to be.\n\nAnd I’ve been missing cycle-count deadlines I normally wouldn’t miss. Then by the time I get home, I’m just drained… not really playing basketball or working on my sketches like I used to. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing I should be coming in for, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not as much when I’m away from work, I don’t think. It’s more like… it starts before I even get there, like I’m already tense walking into the warehouse because of the workflow lead thing. Then I’ll get wound up over small SAP errors, and I hate that because I know it probably sounds minor. \n\nBy the time I’m home I’m mostly just drained, and I haven’t really had the energy for basketball or sketching. So I guess it’s not constant anxiety everywhere, but it’s definitely carrying over some, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes or stressful events in your personal life recently, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I mean, nothing big that I can think of outside of work. Home life is pretty normal, as far as I can tell. I don’t want to overstate it—there hasn’t been, like, a major event or anything. It mostly seems tied to the work change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since you became the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say sad most of the time, no. I mean, I’ve been frustrated and kind of worn out by it, but “sad” feels maybe stronger than what I’d call it. Mostly I just feel tense about work and then tired afterward… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I don’t want to make it sound like I’m refusing to do my job or anything. But since they made me the acting workflow lead, I think I’ve been putting off some of the stuff that feels like it could go wrong.\n\nLike cycle counts—I used to stay on top of those, and now I’ll kind of delay getting into them, and then I end up missing deadlines I wouldn’t have missed before. And with SAP issues, even small errors get me really tense, so I probably avoid dealing with them right away sometimes. I know that’s not ideal.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach issues since becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might get the normal kind of tension now and then, but I haven’t noticed headaches or stomach problems in any real way since taking that role. Nothing I’d want to make a big deal out of, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family since you started feeling this way about work?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, I might be a little quieter when I get home, but I don’t think it’s caused any real problems with friends or family. At least, nobody’s said anything to me. I’m trying not to take work home too much, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of your work-related stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a big way. I mean, sometimes I might lie there thinking about work a little, but I wouldn’t say I’m having real sleep problems because of it. At least nothing that feels serious, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate on things you normally enjoy, like basketball or sketching, when you are doing them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so, a little more than I’d like to admit. I haven’t really been showing up for basketball lately, and I’ve let my usual sketching time kind of disappear. It’s not that I don’t enjoy them anymore, it’s more like work keeps taking over my headspace and my time.\n\nAnd, um… my supervisor did bring up some missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, which was embarrassing. I know that’s not exactly the same as concentrating on hobbies, but it feels related, like I’m not keeping up the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work-related stress is affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is, more than I wanted to admit at first. I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, because it’s not, but work has started taking up a lot of space in my life.\n\nMy supervisor had to bring up those missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, which was hard to hear. And outside of work, I’ve kind of stopped showing up for basketball, and my usual sketching time has pretty much disappeared. It feels like work is sort of taking over, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about what your coworkers or supervisor think of your performance as the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do worry about that some. I try not to, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m doing a terrible job or anything, but when my supervisor brought up the missed cycle-count deadlines in that performance check-in, it kind of stuck with me.\n\nI guess I’ve been concerned they’re noticing that I’m not keeping up the way I usually would. And outside work, I’ve stopped showing up for basketball and haven’t really been sketching like I normally do, so it feels like the job is kind of taking over more than I want it to. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your stress primarily about the responsibilities of being the acting workflow lead or about the possibility of not doing well in that role?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the responsibilities themselves… but, yeah, tied up with not wanting to mess it up, too.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I kind of walk into the warehouse already tense. I try to stay calm, but little things—like SAP errors—have been getting under my skin more than they should. And then missing cycle-count deadlines I didn’t used to miss makes me worry I’m not doing well in the role.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s been following me home. I’m too drained for basketball, and I haven’t really been working on my sketches. So… I guess it’s both, but it started with the added responsibility. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work-related stress is significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is interfering, at least more than I’d like to admit. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function, because I am still going to work and trying to keep things moving, but my supervisor did have to bring up the missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in. That was kind of a wake-up call.\n\nAnd at home, or outside of work, it’s showing up too. I’ve stopped showing up for basketball, and I’m not really taking my usual time to sketch anymore. It feels like work is just… taking over more of my life than it should. I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s kind of been there all the time since they made me acting workflow lead.\n\nI’ll walk into the warehouse already tense, and then little things like SAP errors get to me more than they should. And then when I get home, I’m just drained — I’m not playing basketball like I usually would, and I’m not really working on my sketches either.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say it’s more than just normal work stress at this point. Mostly I just feel tense about work and then tired afterward… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain tasks or situations at work since becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… I don’t want to make it sound like I’m refusing to do my job or anything. But since they made me the acting workflow lead, I think I’ve been putting off some of the stuff that feels like it could go wrong.\n\nLike cycle counts—I used to stay on top of those, and now I’ll kind of delay getting into them, and then I end up missing deadlines I wouldn’t have missed before. And with SAP issues, even small errors get me really tense, so I probably avoid dealing with them right away sometimes. I know that’s not ideal.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach issues since becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might get the normal kind of tension now and then, but I haven’t noticed headaches or stomach problems in any real way since taking that role. Nothing I’d want to make a big deal out of, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family since you started feeling this way about work?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, I might be a little quieter when I get home, but I don’t think it’s caused any real problems with friends or family. At least, nobody’s said anything to me. I’m trying not to take work home too much, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of your work-related stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a big way. I mean, sometimes I might lie there thinking about work a little, but I wouldn’t say I’m having real sleep problems because of it. At least nothing that feels serious, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate on things you normally enjoy, like basketball or sketching, when you are doing them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so, a little more than I’d like to admit. I haven’t really been showing up for basketball lately, and I’ve let my usual sketching time kind of disappear. It’s not that I don’t enjoy them anymore, it’s more like work keeps taking over my headspace and my time.\n\nAnd, um… my supervisor did bring up some missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, which was embarrassing. I know that’s not exactly the same as concentrating on hobbies, but it feels related, like I’m not keeping up the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work-related stress is affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is, more than I wanted to admit at first. I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, because it’s not, but work has started taking up a lot of space in my life.\n\nMy supervisor had to bring up those missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, which was hard to hear. And outside of work, I’ve kind of stopped showing up for basketball, and my usual sketching time has pretty much disappeared. It feels like work is sort of taking over, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about what your coworkers or supervisor think of your performance as the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do worry about that some. I try not to, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m doing a terrible job or anything, but when my supervisor brought up the missed cycle-count deadlines in that performance check-in, it kind of stuck with me.\n\nI guess I’ve been concerned they’re noticing that I’m not keeping up the way I usually would. And outside work, I’ve stopped showing up for basketball and haven’t really been sketching like I normally do, so it feels like the job is kind of taking over more than I want it to. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your stress primarily about the responsibilities of being the acting workflow lead or about the possibility of not doing well in that role?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the responsibilities themselves… but, yeah, tied up with not wanting to mess it up, too.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I kind of walk into the warehouse already tense. I try to stay calm, but little things—like SAP errors—have been getting under my skin more than they should. And then missing cycle-count deadlines I didn’t used to miss makes me worry I’m not doing well in the role.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s been following me home. I’m too drained for basketball, and I haven’t really been working on my sketches. So… I guess it’s both, but it started with the added responsibility. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work-related stress is significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is interfering, at least more than I’d like to admit. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function, because I am still going to work and trying to keep things moving, but my supervisor did have to bring up the missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in. That was kind of a wake-up call.\n\nAnd at home, or outside of work, it’s showing up too. I’ve stopped showing up for basketball, and I’m not really taking my usual time to sketch anymore. It feels like work is just… taking over more of my life than it should. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to fulfill your role as acting workflow lead due to your stress?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m completely unable to do it… I mean, I’m still showing up and trying to handle the role. But it’s definitely affecting me.\n\nMy supervisor did have to bring up the missed cycle-count deadlines during a performance check-in, which was hard to hear. And outside of work, I’ve kind of stopped showing up for basketball and I haven’t been making time to sketch like I usually do, because it feels like work is taking over most of my headspace.\n\nSo… maybe not unable, exactly, but I’m not keeping up the way I normally would. Is that a fair way to put it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work-related stress is causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is causing significant distress. I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s kind of been there all the time since they made me acting workflow lead.\n\nI’ll walk into the warehouse already tense, and then little things like SAP errors get to me more than they should. I’ve missed cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, and that bothers me. And then when I get home, I’m just drained — I’m not playing basketball like I usually would, and I’m not really working on my sketches either.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say it’s more than just normal work stress at this point. Does that sound like what you mean by significant distress?\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social situations or interactions with others because of your work-related stress?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people completely or anything. But yeah, I guess I’ve pulled back some.\n\nI’ve stopped showing up for basketball, which is usually kind of my social time too, and I’m not really taking my usual sketching time either. It’s like work has been taking over my life more than I want to admit. And with my supervisor bringing up the missed cycle-count deadlines, I’ve just felt embarrassed and kind of less like being around people.\n\nSo… not hiding from everyone, exactly, but I am definitely not showing up the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doubting your ability to do your job since becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have started doubting myself a bit. I don’t love admitting that, because I’ve always felt like I was pretty dependable at work.\n\nBut since taking on the acting workflow lead role, I’ve missed some cycle-count deadlines, and my supervisor had to bring that up during a performance check-in. That kind of shook me. It made me wonder if I’m actually handling the job the way I should be.\n\nAnd then outside of work, I’m not really doing the things that usually help me feel like myself — I’ve stopped showing up for basketball and I’m not making time to sketch. It feels like work is taking up so much space in my life that it makes me question whether I’m keeping up okay. Is that… does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific event or situation at work that triggered your stress when you became the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it was really when they made me the acting workflow lead. I don’t know if there was one single blow-up or anything dramatic, but that change kind of started it.\n\nSince then I’ve been walking into the warehouse already tense, and little things like SAP errors bother me more than they probably should. I’ve missed cycle-count deadlines I normally wouldn’t miss, and then I go home just drained — not really playing basketball or even working on my sketches.\n\nSo I guess the situation was taking on that role and feeling like I suddenly had more riding on me. Is that the kind of trigger you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But since they made me the acting workflow lead, I think I’ve been putting off some of the stuff that feels like it could go wrong.\n\nLike cycle counts—I used to stay on top of those, and now I’ll kind of delay getting into them, and then I end up missing deadlines I wouldn’t have missed before. And with SAP issues, even small errors get me really tense, so I probably avoid dealing with them right away sometimes. I know that’s not ideal.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach issues since becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might get the normal kind of tension now and then, but I haven’t noticed headaches or stomach problems in any real way since taking that role. Nothing I’d want to make a big deal out of, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family since you started feeling this way about work?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve really noticed, no. I mean, I might be a little quieter when I get home, but I don’t think it’s caused any real problems with friends or family. At least, nobody’s said anything to me. I’m trying not to take work home too much, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of your work-related stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a big way. I mean, sometimes I might lie there thinking about work a little, but I wouldn’t say I’m having real sleep problems because of it. At least nothing that feels serious, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate on things you normally enjoy, like basketball or sketching, when you are doing them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so, a little more than I’d like to admit. I haven’t really been showing up for basketball lately, and I’ve let my usual sketching time kind of disappear. It’s not that I don’t enjoy them anymore, it’s more like work keeps taking over my headspace and my time.\n\nAnd, um… my supervisor did bring up some missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, which was embarrassing. I know that’s not exactly the same as concentrating on hobbies, but it feels related, like I’m not keeping up the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work-related stress is affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is, more than I wanted to admit at first. I don’t want to make it sound like everything is falling apart, because it’s not, but work has started taking up a lot of space in my life.\n\nMy supervisor had to bring up those missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in, which was hard to hear. And outside of work, I’ve kind of stopped showing up for basketball, and my usual sketching time has pretty much disappeared. It feels like work is sort of taking over, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about what your coworkers or supervisor think of your performance as the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do worry about that some. I try not to, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’m doing a terrible job or anything, but when my supervisor brought up the missed cycle-count deadlines in that performance check-in, it kind of stuck with me.\n\nI guess I’ve been concerned they’re noticing that I’m not keeping up the way I usually would. And outside work, I’ve stopped showing up for basketball and haven’t really been sketching like I normally do, so it feels like the job is kind of taking over more than I want it to. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your stress primarily about the responsibilities of being the acting workflow lead or about the possibility of not doing well in that role?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the responsibilities themselves… but, yeah, tied up with not wanting to mess it up, too.\n\nEver since they made me the acting workflow lead, I kind of walk into the warehouse already tense. I try to stay calm, but little things—like SAP errors—have been getting under my skin more than they should. And then missing cycle-count deadlines I didn’t used to miss makes me worry I’m not doing well in the role.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s been following me home. I’m too drained for basketball, and I haven’t really been working on my sketches. So… I guess it’s both, but it started with the added responsibility. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work-related stress is significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is interfering, at least more than I’d like to admit. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function, because I am still going to work and trying to keep things moving, but my supervisor did have to bring up the missed cycle-count deadlines in a performance check-in. That was kind of a wake-up call.\n\nAnd at home, or outside of work, it’s showing up too. I’ve stopped showing up for basketball, and I’m not really taking my usual time to sketch anymore. It feels like work is just… taking over more of my life than it should. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to fulfill your role as acting workflow lead due to your stress?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m completely unable to do it… I mean, I’m still showing up and trying to handle the role. But it’s definitely affecting me.\n\nMy supervisor did have to bring up the missed cycle-count deadlines during a performance check-in, which was hard to hear. And outside of work, I’ve kind of stopped showing up for basketball and I haven’t been making time to sketch like I usually do, because it feels like work is taking over most of my headspace.\n\nSo… maybe not unable, exactly, but I’m not keeping up the way I normally would. Is that a fair way to put it?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work-related stress is causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is causing significant distress. I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s kind of been there all the time since they made me acting workflow lead.\n\nI’ll walk into the warehouse already tense, and then little things like SAP errors get to me more than they should. I’ve missed cycle-count deadlines I never used to miss, and that bothers me. And then when I get home, I’m just drained — I’m not playing basketball like I usually would, and I’m not really working on my sketches either.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say it’s more than just normal work stress at this point. Does that sound like what you mean by significant distress?\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social situations or interactions with others because of your work-related stress?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding people completely or anything. But yeah, I guess I’ve pulled back some.\n\nI’ve stopped showing up for basketball, which is usually kind of my social time too, and I’m not really taking my usual sketching time either. It’s like work has been taking over my life more than I want to admit. And with my supervisor bringing up the missed cycle-count deadlines, I’ve just felt embarrassed and kind of less like being around people.\n\nSo… not hiding from everyone, exactly, but I am definitely not showing up the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doubting your ability to do your job since becoming the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have started doubting myself a bit. I don’t love admitting that, because I’ve always felt like I was pretty dependable at work.\n\nBut since taking on the acting workflow lead role, I’ve missed some cycle-count deadlines, and my supervisor had to bring that up during a performance check-in. That kind of shook me. It made me wonder if I’m actually handling the job the way I should be.\n\nAnd then outside of work, I’m not really doing the things that usually help me feel like myself — I’ve stopped showing up for basketball and I’m not making time to sketch. It feels like work is taking up so much space in my life that it makes me question whether I’m keeping up okay. Is that… does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific event or situation at work that triggered your stress when you became the acting workflow lead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it was really when they made me the acting workflow lead. I don’t know if there was one single blow-up or anything dramatic, but that change kind of started it.\n\nSince then I’ve been walking into the warehouse already tense, and little things like SAP errors bother me more than they probably should. I’ve missed cycle-count deadlines I normally wouldn’t miss, and then I go home just drained — not really playing basketball or even working on my sketches.\n\nSo I guess the situation was taking on that role and feeling like I suddenly had more riding on me. Is that the kind of trigger you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms started immediately after you were made the acting workflow lead or was there a delay?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was, like, the very next day or anything. The lead role started after the team merger, and at first I tried to tell myself it was just normal adjustment stuff.\n\nBut within the first month or two, I could tell I wasn’t handling work the way I normally do. I guess I didn’t want to make a big deal of it, but looking back, that’s when it started to feel noticeable. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: How long after becoming the acting workflow lead did you first notice these symptoms?\n\nPatient: I think it was within the first month or two after I stepped into the lead role. It started after the team merger, and at first I kind of brushed it off as normal adjustment, but after a little while I could tell I wasn’t handling work the way I usually do.\n\nI hope that makes sense — it wasn’t immediate, but it also wasn’t months and months later.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
